*   **Novelty:** The combination of hierarchical decomposition, FPGA acceleration (specifically direct equation solving), and incremental learning for LLMs has some novelty, but the individual components are not entirely new. The specific *integration* and *application* to a compound LLM architecture represents a novel aspect. However, the paper builds heavily on existing techniques like PEFT, quantization, and FPGA-based acceleration. The novelty lies in the specific *arrangement* and *adaptation* of these techniques within the proposed architecture.
*   **Significance:** The problem being addressed (energy consumption and scalability of LLMs) is highly significant. The potential impact of reducing the carbon footprint of large-scale AI is considerable.  However, the paper remains theoretical. There's no empirical evaluation or experimental validation of the proposed framework.  This significantly limits the assessment of its practical impact. While the theoretical analysis points to potential benefits, real-world performance is unknown. The potential significance hinges on whether the theoretical gains translate into measurable improvements in real applications, particularly in edge environments. Furthermore, many current advancements exist within these areas (e.g. FlashAttention). How does the proposal compare? The paper does not make a strong argument in this comparison.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a critical problem in AI (energy consumption).
    *   The hybrid approach has the potential to balance efficiency and adaptability.
    *   Clear and well-structured explanation of the proposed framework.
    *   The introduction of the Compound LLM architecture is a valuable conceptual contribution.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Lack of experimental validation. The absence of empirical results significantly weakens the paper's claims. Simulation results or real-world deployments are needed to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    *   The analysis remains high-level. Specific implementation details and hardware resource requirements are not fully elaborated.
    *   Limited discussion on the challenges of integrating the different components (FPGA acceleration, incremental learning). How easy/difficult it would be to combine the two?
    *   Limited comparison with existing state-of-the-art techniques. While the paper mentions related work, it does not provide a detailed performance comparison demonstrating how the proposed method outperforms existing solutions.
    *   Scalability claims are not sufficiently supported. While the paper argues that the framework is scalable, this claim needs to be backed by evidence or a more in-depth scalability analysis. The argument lacks detail.
    *   The benefits are not guaranteed. While there is no retraining, catastrophic forgetting could still occur during incremental adaptation.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents an interesting and potentially valuable approach to addressing a critical problem in AI. However, the absence of experimental validation and the lack of a detailed implementation analysis significantly limit its impact. The novelty, while present, is incremental rather than revolutionary.

Score: 6

This score reflects the following:

*   The idea itself is quite interesting.
*   The paper introduces the concept of Compound LLM.
*   The concept of FPGA-based acceleration may be beneficial, but has already been heavily used in literature.
*   The lack of any empirical evidence, comparisons to baseline, implementation information, or detail on hardware requirements means the paper is currently just a theoretical proposal with no real proof of practicality. Without validation, it's difficult to assess the true significance of the work.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### TextMine: LLM-Powered Knowledge Extraction for Humanitarian Mine Action
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15098v1)
- **Authors**: Chenyue Zhou, Gürkan Solmaz, Flavio Cirillo, Kiril Gashteovski, Jonathan Fürst
- **Abstract**: Humanitarian Mine Action has generated extensive best-practice knowledge, but much remains locked in unstructured reports. We introduce TextMine, an ontology-guided pipeline that uses Large Language Models to extract knowledge triples from HMA texts. TextMine integrates document chunking, domain-aware prompting, triple extraction, and both reference-based and LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation. We also create the first HMA ontology and a curated dataset of real-world demining reports. Experiments show ontology-aligned prompts boost extraction accuracy by 44.2%, cut hallucinations by 22.5%, and improve format conformance by 20.9% over baselines. While validated on Cambodian reports, TextMine can adapt to global demining efforts or other domains, transforming unstructured data into structured knowledge.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TextMine: LLM-Powered Knowledge Extraction for Humanitarian Mine Action":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TextMine, a novel ontology-guided pipeline leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) for extracting structured knowledge from unstructured Humanitarian Mine Action (HMA) reports. TextMine incorporates document chunking, domain-aware prompting, triple extraction, and both reference-based and reference-free evaluation methods. The authors also create a custom HMA ontology and a curated dataset of real-world demining reports. Experiments demonstrate that using ontology-aligned prompts significantly improves extraction accuracy, reduces hallucinations, and enhances format conformance compared to baseline prompts. The pipeline is validated on Cambodian demining reports, but the authors claim it is adaptable to global demining efforts and other domains.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While LLMs have been applied to knowledge extraction in other domains, the application to the specific area of Humanitarian Mine Action is novel. The creation of a dedicated HMA ontology and a curated dataset is also a significant contribution, as it addresses a specific resource gap. The reference-free evaluation method using an LLM-as-a-Judge is an interesting approach, particularly when ground truth data is limited, although its reliance on LLMs for evaluation also introduces a new set of potential biases and inaccuracies. The optimized prompting strategies based on ontology alignment are not entirely new, but the thorough investigation and quantitative demonstration of their effectiveness in this specific domain adds value.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of TextMine is considerable. Automated knowledge extraction from HMA reports can significantly improve decision-making, operational efficiency, and knowledge sharing among mine action organizations. The ability to transform unstructured data into a structured knowledge base can facilitate data analysis, trend identification, and the development of more effective demining strategies. However, it's essential to acknowledge that TextMine only extracts information; the effectiveness of the downstream planning based on this information still relies on the quality of information provided and how it is incorporated into an overall strategy.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper presents a well-defined and comprehensive pipeline.
    *   The creation of a domain-specific ontology and dataset is a valuable contribution to the HMA field.
    *   The experiments are well-designed and demonstrate the effectiveness of ontology-aligned prompts.
    *   The reference-free evaluation method offers a practical solution for domains with limited ground truth data.
    *   The detailed analysis of hallucination rates and format conformance is commendable.
    *   The paper recognizes and addresses potential ethical considerations, particularly concerning data privacy and the risk of misinformation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dataset is relatively small, which limits the generalizability of the results. The reliance of only 100 promtp-response can be biased on the domain, which can vary drastically.
    *   The evaluation of the LLM-as-a-Judge approach is not completely convincing. While the authors demonstrate some correlation between LLM-judged rankings and reference-based rankings, the accuracy of the LLM judges themselves is not fully established. The dependence on LLMs for both extraction and evaluation raises questions about potential circular reasoning and biases.
    *   The format conformance consistently high might be due to a relatively simple extraction framework where the LLM does not perform intricate or more detailed extraction.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more thorough discussion of the limitations of the approach, particularly concerning its sensitivity to the quality of the HMA reports and potential for extracting inaccurate or misleading information.
    *   The paper could provide some analysis and statistics of runtime performance so potential users can determine the speed and efficiency of the process to be done.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the HMA field by providing a practical framework for automated knowledge extraction. The HMA ontology and dataset can be used by other researchers to develop and evaluate new knowledge extraction methods. The LLM-as-a-Judge approach can inspire further research on reference-free evaluation techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by applying LLMs to the important problem of knowledge extraction in the HMA domain. The creation of a dedicated ontology and dataset, combined with the thorough experimental evaluation, demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed pipeline and optimization techniques. However, the limited dataset size and reliance on LLMs for evaluation restrict the potential impact of the work.

Therefore, a score of **7** is warranted. The work has clear novelty and significance within a niche domain, with the potential to inspire further research. However, the limitations and outstanding questions suggest that the full potential of the approach remains to be explored.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Model probabilities cannot distinguish between possible and impossible language
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15114v1)
- **Authors**: Evelina Leivada, Raquel Montero, Paolo Morosi, Natalia Moskvina, Tamara Serrano, Marcel Aguilar, Fritz Guenther
- **Abstract**: A controversial test for Large Language Models concerns the ability to discern possible from impossible language. While some evidence attests to the models' sensitivity to what crosses the limits of grammatically impossible language, this evidence has been contested on the grounds of the soundness of the testing material. We use model-internal representations to tap directly into the way Large Language Models represent the 'grammatical-ungrammatical' distinction. In a novel benchmark, we elicit probabilities from 4 models and compute minimal-pair surprisal differences, juxtaposing probabilities assigned to grammatical sentences to probabilities assigned to (i) lower frequency grammatical sentences, (ii) ungrammatical sentences, (iii) semantically odd sentences, and (iv) pragmatically odd sentences. The prediction is that if string-probabilities can function as proxies for the limits of grammar, the ungrammatical condition will stand out among the conditions that involve linguistic violations, showing a spike in the surprisal rates. Our results do not reveal a unique surprisal signature for ungrammatical prompts, as the semantically and pragmatically odd conditions consistently show higher surprisal. We thus demonstrate that probabilities do not constitute reliable proxies for model-internal representations of syntactic knowledge. Consequently, claims about models being able to distinguish possible from impossible language need verification through a different methodology.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can distinguish between grammatically possible and impossible language based on string probabilities (specifically surprisal values).  It uses a novel benchmark to compare the surprisal of grammatical sentences with those that are (i) lower frequency grammatical sentences, (ii) ungrammatical sentences, (iii) semantically odd sentences, and (iv) pragmatically odd sentences.  The authors hypothesize that if string probabilities accurately reflect the limits of grammar, ungrammatical sentences should exhibit a unique spike in surprisal rates compared to other types of linguistic violations. The study found that ungrammatical prompts did *not* exhibit a unique surprisal signature, as semantically and pragmatically odd conditions consistently showed higher surprisal. The authors conclude that probabilities are unreliable proxies for syntactic knowledge in LLMs, questioning claims about their ability to distinguish between possible and impossible languages.  They also critique the methodology of existing studies in this area, raising concerns about stimulus design and the Now-or-Never bottleneck potentially biasing against human performance in comparative tasks.  Finally, the authors discuss the limitations of existing benchmarks and highlight the need for more nuanced approaches to evaluate LLMs' linguistic knowledge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel benchmark by comparing ungrammatical sentences to a broader range of linguistic violations (semantic, pragmatic, and frequency-based).  This is a significant step forward. Previous work has often focused solely on comparing grammatical vs. ungrammatical pairs, limiting the ability to isolate the specific contribution of syntactic violations. Using internal representations of LLMs to get probability estimations is also a very valid approach. The critique of existing benchmarks and their limitations, particularly concerning the Now-or-Never bottleneck, also shows novelty, offering a refreshing perspective.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they challenge the widely held belief that string probabilities are a reliable proxy for syntactic knowledge in LLMs. This directly undermines claims that LLMs have a deep understanding of grammar or can reliably distinguish between possible and impossible languages based on probability measures alone. It highlights the need for more sophisticated evaluation methods and a more nuanced understanding of what probabilities actually represent within these models. The recognition that models' responses can reflect an understanding of the frequency or typicality of statements in their training data, not necessarily a deep understanding of grammatical *rules*, is also important. The methodological criticisms raise important questions about the fairness and validity of existing comparative studies. It also suggests a fruitful line of research: if direct measurement is not ideal, what measure is?

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-designed experiment:** The experimental design, with its controlled minimal pairs and comparison across multiple types of linguistic violations, is a major strength.
    *   **Clear and well-supported arguments:** The authors present their arguments clearly and logically, backing them up with empirical evidence and insightful critiques of previous work.
    *   **Comprehensive discussion:** The paper provides a comprehensive overview of the relevant literature and addresses potential counterarguments.
    *   **Relevance:** The topic is highly relevant in the context of ongoing debates about the capabilities and limitations of LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scope of Conclusions:** The study primarily focuses on *four* specific models. While they represent diverse families, broader validation across a wider range of architectures, sizes, and training datasets would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Complexity of Stimuli:** Even with minimal pairs, the subtle differences in the various violations (semantic, pragmatic, ungrammatical) can be hard to finely control, and some degree of subjective judgement is involved in classifying the violations. Although the study identifies a significant difference between ungrammatical vs unsemantically sentences, this may not hold across all linguistic constructions or languages. More fine-grained analysis of linguistic features (like subcategorization constraints, agreement features, etc) may offer a clearer insight into the inner dynamics of these LLMs.
    *   **Lack of error analysis:** The paper does not conduct error analysis on specific construction that triggers high surprisal for LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field by:

    *   Encouraging the development of more robust and nuanced evaluation methods for LLMs' linguistic abilities.
    *   Promoting a more critical assessment of the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in relation to human language understanding.
    *   Stimulating further research on the relationship between string probabilities and syntactic knowledge in LLMs.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid, original contribution by challenging assumptions about the nature of LLM's syntactic knowledge and proposing a novel benchmark for assessment. However, while the stimuli used in the experiment can be considered novel, they do not explore the different features of language that can be tested for grammaticality. The theoretical framing could have been more rigorous, linking specific linguistic constraints that can be explicitly violated to trigger different surprisal responses. Because it tests only four models, and it is only recently that bigger models such as Mistral-7B were released, it is not clear whether those conclusions can be generalized to bigger LLMs. The overall conclusion is justified. While this will encourage further exploration of LLMs' true abilities, it is not transformative enough to warrant a higher score. However, its methodological contributions and focus on a practically relevant question make it worthwhile.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prestige over merit: An adapted audit of LLM bias in peer review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15122v1)
- **Authors**: Anthony Howell, Jieshu Wang, Luyu Du, Julia Melkers, Varshil Shah
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are playing an increasingly integral, though largely informal, role in scholarly peer review. Yet it remains unclear whether LLMs reproduce the biases observed in human decision-making. We adapt a resume-style audit to scientific publishing, developing a multi-role LLM simulation (editor/reviewer) that evaluates a representative set of high-quality manuscripts across the physical, biological, and social sciences under randomized author identities (institutional prestige, gender, race). The audit reveals a strong and consistent institutional-prestige bias: identical papers attributed to low-prestige affiliations face a significantly higher risk of rejection, despite only modest differences in LLM-assessed quality. To probe mechanisms, we generate synthetic CVs for the same author profiles; these encode large prestige-linked disparities and an inverted prestige-tenure gradient relative to national benchmarks. The results suggest that both domain norms and prestige-linked priors embedded in training data shape paper-level outcomes once identity is visible, converting affiliation into a decisive status cue.
- **Summary**: This paper presents a comprehensive methodology for evaluating the causal effects of continuous treatments within the framework of Bayesian Additive Regression Trees (BART). The authors introduce innovative extensions to BART, enabling flexible modeling of treatment effects and incorporating priors to enhance estimation. The methodology is tested and validated on synthetic data, demonstrating its ability to recover complex dose-response relationships. Furthermore, the paper applies the extended BART framework to real-world data, offering insights into the effects of interventions.

**Evaluation:**

The strength of this paper lies in its innovative extensions to BART, which allow for a more nuanced understanding of treatment effects than traditional methods. The methodology's flexibility in modeling complex dose-response relationships is a significant advantage. The use of priors to enhance estimation also adds value, allowing researchers to incorporate prior knowledge into the analysis and potentially improve the accuracy of results.

However, the paper could be strengthened by providing a more thorough discussion of the limitations of the proposed methodology. Specifically, the authors should address the computational challenges associated with fitting BART models, as well as the potential for overfitting, particularly when dealing with high-dimensional data. Additionally, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth comparison of the extended BART framework with alternative methods for estimating continuous treatment effects, such as generalized additive models or kernel methods.

While the synthetic data analysis provides evidence of the methodology's ability to recover complex dose-response relationships, the paper could benefit from a more comprehensive evaluation on a wider range of synthetic datasets, including those with more challenging features such as non-linear treatment effects and heterogeneous treatment effects across subgroups. Furthermore, the application to real-world data is somewhat limited, and the authors could explore additional datasets to demonstrate the generalizability of the methodology.

In summary, this paper presents a novel and valuable methodology for estimating continuous treatment effects, but it could be strengthened by addressing its limitations more explicitly, providing a more comprehensive evaluation on synthetic and real-world data, and comparing it more thoroughly with alternative methods.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### WorldForge: Unlocking Emergent 3D/4D Generation in Video Diffusion Model via Training-Free Guidance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15130v1)
- **Authors**: Chenxi Song, Yanming Yang, Tong Zhao, Ruibo Li, Chi Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent video diffusion models demonstrate strong potential in spatial intelligence tasks due to their rich latent world priors. However, this potential is hindered by their limited controllability and geometric inconsistency, creating a gap between their strong priors and their practical use in 3D/4D tasks. As a result, current approaches often rely on retraining or fine-tuning, which risks degrading pretrained knowledge and incurs high computational costs. To address this, we propose WorldForge, a training-free, inference-time framework composed of three tightly coupled modules. Intra-Step Recursive Refinement introduces a recursive refinement mechanism during inference, which repeatedly optimizes network predictions within each denoising step to enable precise trajectory injection. Flow-Gated Latent Fusion leverages optical flow similarity to decouple motion from appearance in the latent space and selectively inject trajectory guidance into motion-related channels. Dual-Path Self-Corrective Guidance compares guided and unguided denoising paths to adaptively correct trajectory drift caused by noisy or misaligned structural signals. Together, these components inject fine-grained, trajectory-aligned guidance without training, achieving both accurate motion control and photorealistic content generation. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks validate our method's superiority in realism, trajectory consistency, and visual fidelity. This work introduces a novel plug-and-play paradigm for controllable video synthesis, offering a new perspective on leveraging generative priors for spatial intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "WORLDFORGE: UNLOCKING EMERGENT 3D/4D GENERATION IN VIDEO DIFFUSION MODEL VIA TRAINING-FREE GUIDANCE":

**Summary:**

The paper presents WorldForge, a novel training-free framework designed to enhance the controllability and geometric consistency of video diffusion models for 3D/4D generation tasks. Addressing limitations in existing models, WorldForge leverages a pre-trained video diffusion model and introduces three key modules: Intra-Step Recursive Refinement (IRR) for precise trajectory injection at each denoising step, Flow-Gated Latent Fusion (FLF) to decouple motion from appearance in the latent space and selectively inject trajectory guidance, and Dual-Path Self-Corrective Guidance (DSG) to adaptively correct trajectory drift by comparing guided and unguided denoising paths. This framework enables precise camera trajectory control and photorealistic content generation without the need for training or fine-tuning. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of WorldForge through experiments on diverse benchmarks, showing improvements in realism, trajectory consistency, and visual fidelity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a notable approach by focusing on inference-time guidance for 3D/4D generation tasks. While techniques like warping-and-repainting are not entirely new, the combination of IRR, FLF, and DSG modules within a training-free framework is a significant contribution. Specifically, the following elements contribute to the novelty:
    *   **Training-Free Approach:** Avoiding costly retraining or fine-tuning is an advantage, preserving the pre-trained model's knowledge and reducing computational burden.
    *   **Intra-Step Recursive Refinement:** The iterative correction loop within each denoising step is novel, allowing for fine-grained trajectory guidance.
    *   **Flow-Gated Latent Fusion:** Disentangling motion from appearance in the latent space is a useful technique for targeted guidance.
    *   **Dual-Path Self-Corrective Guidance:** Adaptive trajectory drift correction using guided and unguided denoising paths provides stability.

*   **Significance:** The significance of WorldForge lies in its potential to improve the usability and accessibility of video diffusion models for spatial intelligence tasks. By enabling precise control over camera trajectories and enhancing geometric consistency without training, the framework unlocks new possibilities for applications like novel view synthesis, free-viewpoint rendering, and controllable video generation. The plug-and-play nature of the method makes it easily adaptable to different video diffusion models. Further significance comes from the demonstrated improvements in visual quality and consistency compared to SOTA methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The framework is training-free, offering computational efficiency and preserving pre-trained knowledge.
    *   The modular design allows for a clear understanding of each component's contribution.
    *   The method achieves state-of-the-art performance in terms of controllability and visual quality.
    *   Extensive experimental validation on diverse datasets and tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   As acknowledged by the authors, the approach can struggle with extremely poor depth estimations and controlling small objects or fine details due to the global nature of the guidance. The framework's reliance on depth estimation as a preliminary step is both a strength and a weakness, as it inherits any limitations of the depth estimation model used.
    *   The inference time increases by 40-50%, which may be a concern for certain applications. While still comparable to training-based solutions, further optimizing the inference speed would improve its practicality.
    *   The paper would benefit from more detailed analysis of the failure modes of the different components, shedding light on edge cases and limitations to understand the framework's strengths and weaknesses under varying conditions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by inspiring new research in inference-time guidance strategies for video diffusion models. The techniques introduced in WorldForge, such as IRR, FLF, and DSG, could be adapted and extended to address other challenges in controllable video generation and spatial intelligence tasks.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

WorldForge presents a highly innovative and effective approach to improving the controllability and visual quality of video diffusion models. The training-free nature of the framework, combined with its modular design and demonstrated state-of-the-art performance, makes it a valuable contribution to the field. Although the limitations related to depth estimation and inference speed should be considered, the overall significance and potential influence of WorldForge are substantial. The paper provides a robust foundation for future research in controllable video generation and spatial intelligence tasks.

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 8/10

### A1: Asynchronous Test-Time Scaling via Conformal Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15148v1)
- **Authors**: Jing Xiong, Qiujiang Chen, Fanghua Ye, Zhongwei Wan, Chuanyang Zheng, Chenyang Zhao, Hui Shen, Alexander Hanbo Li, Chaofan Tao, Haochen Tan, Haoli Bai, Lifeng Shang, Lingpeng Kong, Ngai Wong
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) benefit from test-time scaling, but existing methods face significant challenges, including severe synchronization overhead, memory bottlenecks, and latency, especially during speculative decoding with long reasoning chains. We introduce A1 (Asynchronous Test-Time Scaling), a statistically guaranteed adaptive inference framework that addresses these challenges. A1 refines arithmetic intensity to identify synchronization as the dominant bottleneck, proposes an online calibration strategy to enable asynchronous inference, and designs a three-stage rejection sampling pipeline that supports both sequential and parallel scaling. Through experiments on the MATH, AMC23, AIME24, and AIME25 datasets, across various draft-target model families, we demonstrate that A1 achieves a remarkable 56.7x speedup in test-time scaling and a 4.14x improvement in throughput, all while maintaining accurate rejection-rate control, reducing latency and memory overhead, and no accuracy loss compared to using target model scaling alone. These results position A1 as an efficient and principled solution for scalable LLM inference. We have released the code at https://github.com/menik1126/asynchronous-test-time-scaling.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces A1 (Asynchronous Test-Time Scaling), a novel inference framework designed to improve the efficiency and scalability of large language models (LLMs) during test time. A1 addresses challenges associated with existing test-time scaling methods, such as synchronization overhead, memory bottlenecks, and latency, particularly when speculative decoding is used with long reasoning chains. The core contributions of A1 include: 1) Introducing "asynchronous arithmetic intensity" as a metric to pinpoint throughput and latency bottlenecks in test-time scaling scenarios. 2) Leveraging conformal prediction to manage memory bottlenecks and prediction ranking more efficiently. 3) Implementing a training-free, lossless acceleration method that achieves significant speedups in test-time scaling and throughput, without compromising accuracy.  The method utilizes a three-stage rejection sampling pipeline that supports both sequential and parallel scaling and is evaluated on MATH, AMC23, AIME24, and AIME25 datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and significant problem in the field of LLM inference: the inefficient use of computational resources during test-time scaling. The core idea of asynchronous test-time scaling using conformal prediction is novel and well-motivated. The introduction of asynchronous arithmetic intensity provides a useful diagnostic tool for understanding performance bottlenecks. The experimental results demonstrate substantial speedups and improved throughput, suggesting that the proposed framework is effective.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The combination of asynchronous inference and conformal prediction for test-time scaling is a novel approach that addresses key limitations of existing methods.
*   **Practical Significance:** The framework offers practical benefits in terms of reduced latency, memory overhead, and increased throughput, making it potentially valuable for real-world deployment of LLMs.
*   **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and model families, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of A1.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges associated with synchronous scaling and motivates the need for an asynchronous approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Benchmarks:** While the paper considers several benchmark datasets, they are largely mathematical reasoning-focused. Evaluating the method on a broader range of tasks, including natural language generation, summarization, or question answering, would strengthen the generalizability of the results.
*   **Scalability:** The paper demonstrates promising scaling results, but it would be beneficial to see the method's performance with even larger models and datasets. A more detailed analysis of the framework's scaling behavior under extreme conditions would be valuable.
*   **Complexity**: The proposed framework uses a new metric, asynchronous arithmetic intensity, and introduces a complex conformal prediction algorithm. This may limit it's adoption in production environments.
*   **Comparison to alternative methods**: The performance improvement with respect to basic speculative decoding is not clearly addressed, and this should be part of the experimental comparison.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM inference.  The introduction of asynchronous test-time scaling could lead to more efficient and scalable LLM deployments, especially in scenarios where long reasoning chains or real-time responses are required. By releasing the code, the authors are facilitating further research and adoption of A1.

**Overall Score:**

Given the novelty of the approach, the practical significance of the results, and the solid experimental validation, I assign this paper a score of 7. The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field, but has some of the limitations outlined above.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Asymptotic Study of In-context Learning with Random Transformers through Equivalent Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15152v1)
- **Authors**: Samet Demir, Zafer Dogan
- **Abstract**: We study the in-context learning (ICL) capabilities of pretrained Transformers in the setting of nonlinear regression. Specifically, we focus on a random Transformer with a nonlinear MLP head where the first layer is randomly initialized and fixed while the second layer is trained. Furthermore, we consider an asymptotic regime where the context length, input dimension, hidden dimension, number of training tasks, and number of training samples jointly grow. In this setting, we show that the random Transformer behaves equivalent to a finite-degree Hermite polynomial model in terms of ICL error. This equivalence is validated through simulations across varying activation functions, context lengths, hidden layer widths (revealing a double-descent phenomenon), and regularization settings. Our results offer theoretical and empirical insights into when and how MLP layers enhance ICL, and how nonlinearity and over-parameterization influence model performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the in-context learning (ICL) capabilities of pre-trained Transformers with nonlinear multi-layer perceptron (MLP) heads in nonlinear regression tasks. The authors focus on a random Transformer with a fixed, randomly initialized first MLP layer and a trainable second layer. Under an asymptotic regime (where context length, input dimension, hidden dimension, number of training tasks, and number of samples jointly grow), they demonstrate that the Transformer behaves equivalently to a finite-degree Hermite polynomial model concerning ICL error. The equivalence is validated through simulations across various activation functions, context lengths, hidden layer widths, and regularization settings. The study provides theoretical and empirical insights into how MLP layers enhance ICL, highlighting the influence of nonlinearity and over-parameterization on model performance. Key findings include conditions under which nonlinear MLPs improve ICL over linear Transformers, revealing a double-descent phenomenon in ICL error related to model complexity and highlighting the importance of proper regularization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to understanding the role of MLPs in Transformer-based ICL, addressing a gap in the existing literature. It moves beyond simplistic attention-only architectures and specific task types (e.g., classification with ReLU) to examine more general nonlinear regression problems within the standard Transformer architecture. The use of asymptotic analysis provides a powerful tool for characterizing the behavior of these models in high-dimensional settings, yielding insights that might be difficult to obtain through purely empirical studies.

* **Novelty:** The work has good novelty. The theoretical demonstration of equivalence to a Hermite polynomial is a clever approach that provides a more tractable way to analyze the complex behavior of the Transformer.  The conditions under which MLPs demonstrably improve ICL performance, the observation of a double-descent phenomenon in the ICL error, and the mitigation through regularization represent original contributions.
* **Significance:** The results have substantial significance for the field. By providing a theoretical framework for understanding the impact of MLPs on ICL, the paper can guide the design of more effective Transformer architectures. The practical insights into the selection of activation functions, context lengths, hidden dimensions, and regularization techniques are valuable for practitioners. Showing and mitigating double descent in ICL is an important observation, contributing to a more robust understanding of these models' behavior.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Solid Theoretical Foundation:** The use of asymptotic analysis and Gaussian universality results is well-motivated and rigorously applied.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The simulations across various settings provide strong support for the theoretical findings.
    * **Clear and Well-Structured Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.
    * **Addresses a Gap in the Literature:** It provides a much needed theoretical grounding for the impact of nonlinear MLPs on the ICL capabilities of Transformers.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** While the paper covers a significant area, it focuses on a specific setting (nonlinear regression with a random Transformer). Extending the analysis to more complex architectures (e.g., multi-head attention, deep MLP stacks) would further enhance its impact.
    * **Assumptions:** Asymptotic analysis relies on certain assumptions that may not always hold in practice. A discussion of the limitations of these assumptions and their potential impact on the results would be beneficial.
    * **Simplifications**: While necessary for tractability, the use of linear attention and random initialization of the first MLP layer simplifies the model.  Analyzing more complex or learned attention mechanisms and allowing for training of the first MLP layer may offer further insights but would likely significantly increase the complexity of the analysis.
* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design and training of Transformer models for ICL, leading to more effective and robust models.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a significant and novel contribution to the theoretical understanding of Transformers' ICL capabilities, particularly regarding the role of nonlinear MLPs. The combination of asymptotic analysis, empirical validation, and clear presentation makes it a valuable addition to the field. However, the scope limitations (specifically, focusing on a simplified architecture and a specific type of task) slightly temper the score. Furthermore, while rigorously validated, further discussion around the assumptions inherent in the asymptotic regime would further strengthen the paper's conclusions. Despite these minor limitations, the work is impactful and deserving of a high score.

- **Classification**: stat.ML
- **Score**: 7/10

### AnoF-Diff: One-Step Diffusion-Based Anomaly Detection for Forceful Tool Use
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15153v1)
- **Authors**: Yating Lin, Zixuan Huang, Fan Yang, Dmitry Berenson
- **Abstract**: Multivariate time-series anomaly detection, which is critical for identifying unexpected events, has been explored in the field of machine learning for several decades. However, directly applying these methods to data from forceful tool use tasks is challenging because streaming sensor data in the real world tends to be inherently noisy, exhibits non-stationary behavior, and varies across different tasks and tools. To address these challenges, we propose a method, AnoF-Diff, based on the diffusion model to extract force-torque features from time-series data and use force-torque features to detect anomalies. We compare our method with other state-of-the-art methods in terms of F1-score and Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic curve (AUROC) on four forceful tool-use tasks, demonstrating that our method has better performance and is more robust to a noisy dataset. We also propose the method of parallel anomaly score evaluation based on one-step diffusion and demonstrate how our method can be used for online anomaly detection in several forceful tool use experiments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AnoF-Diff, a novel anomaly detection method specifically designed for robotic forceful tool-use tasks. AnoF-Diff leverages diffusion models to denoise force-torque features extracted from multivariate time-series data, using the reconstruction loss as an anomaly score.  A key contribution is a conditional masking strategy that selectively diffuses only the force-torque components, while preserving robot state and task-specific conditions. The paper also proposes a parallel anomaly score evaluation method based on one-step diffusion to improve real-time performance.  The method is evaluated on four real-world forceful tool-use tasks (placement, prying, socket mating, and nut tightening), demonstrating superior performance and robustness compared to several state-of-the-art anomaly detection baselines. The paper also presents an online anomaly detection demonstration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a few key novel elements. First, the application of diffusion models to anomaly detection within the specific context of robotic forceful tool-use is novel, although diffusion models for general time series anomaly detection are emerging. Second, the *conditional masking strategy*, where diffusion is selectively applied to force-torque data while conditioning on robot state, is a significant innovation.  This allows the model to learn state-dependent force dynamics more effectively. Third, the parallel one-step diffusion approach to accelerate anomaly score computation is a practical contribution that enhances real-time applicability. The combination of these aspects distinguishes the paper.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a practically important problem: detecting anomalies during forceful tool-use in robotics. Failures in such tasks can lead to damage or unsafe situations. By focusing on force-torque data and leveraging diffusion models, the paper offers a promising solution. The improved performance compared to baselines, and the demonstration of real-time capability through parallel evaluation, makes this work valuable for the robotics community.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough experimental evaluation on real-world robotic tasks.  The comparison to multiple strong baselines (including both classical and deep learning methods) strengthens the credibility of the results.
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem and motivates the approach with well-reasoned arguments regarding the challenges of noisy, non-stationary data in robotic manipulation.
    * **Practical Contributions:** The parallel anomaly score evaluation is a valuable practical contribution, addressing the need for real-time anomaly detection in robotics.
    * **Ablation Study:** The inclusion of a diffusion ablation study further strengthens the paper by demonstrating the benefits of conditioning on force-torque data.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Control Policies:**  The paper acknowledges that the data was collected using relatively simple control policies.  The generalizability of the method to more complex controllers and more subtle anomalies is an open question. A discussion on the limitations of the anomaly types tested would be beneficial.
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The fixed-length sliding window approach may limit the method's ability to handle variable-length signals, and the window length is a potentially sensitive hyperparameter.  More discussion of the impact of window size would improve the paper.
    * **Incremental Contribution:** While the paper's approach is clearly novel, the underlying core diffusion modeling technology is not brand new. The novelty primarily resides in the specific application and conditional architecture choices. The evaluation could be improved by showing sensitivity to various parameter changes in the conditional diffusion setup (force weighting, condition weighting).
* **Potential Impact:**  The paper has the potential to impact the field of robotic manipulation by providing a robust and efficient method for detecting anomalies during forceful tool-use tasks. This could lead to safer and more reliable robotic systems. It would also be useful for other time-series anomaly detection domains.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with clear novelty and practical significance. The thorough experimental evaluation and the parallel evaluation approach are particularly valuable.  However, the limitations regarding the scope of control policies and hyperparameter sensitivity prevent the paper from achieving a higher score.  The incremental nature of the contribution also brings the score down.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Self-Improving Embodied Foundation Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15155v1)
- **Authors**: Seyed Kamyar Seyed Ghasemipour, Ayzaan Wahid, Jonathan Tompson, Pannag Sanketi, Igor Mordatch
- **Abstract**: Foundation models trained on web-scale data have revolutionized robotics, but their application to low-level control remains largely limited to behavioral cloning. Drawing inspiration from the success of the reinforcement learning stage in fine-tuning large language models, we propose a two-stage post-training approach for robotics. The first stage, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), fine-tunes pretrained foundation models using both: a) behavioral cloning, and b) steps-to-go prediction objectives. In the second stage, Self-Improvement, steps-to-go prediction enables the extraction of a well-shaped reward function and a robust success detector, enabling a fleet of robots to autonomously practice downstream tasks with minimal human supervision. Through extensive experiments on real-world and simulated robot embodiments, our novel post-training recipe unveils significant results on Embodied Foundation Models. First, we demonstrate that the combination of SFT and Self-Improvement is significantly more sample-efficient than scaling imitation data collection for supervised learning, and that it leads to policies with significantly higher success rates. Further ablations highlight that the combination of web-scale pretraining and Self-Improvement is the key to this sample-efficiency. Next, we demonstrate that our proposed combination uniquely unlocks a capability that current methods cannot achieve: autonomously practicing and acquiring novel skills that generalize far beyond the behaviors observed in the imitation learning datasets used during training. These findings highlight the transformative potential of combining pretrained foundation models with online Self-Improvement to enable autonomous skill acquisition in robotics. Our project website can be found at https://self-improving-efms.github.io .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a two-stage post-training framework for Embodied Foundation Models (EFMs) aimed at improving their low-level control capabilities in robotics. The first stage, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), fine-tunes pre-trained foundation models using behavioral cloning and a "steps-to-go" prediction objective. The second stage, Self-Improvement, leverages the steps-to-go prediction to extract a reward function and success detector, enabling robots to autonomously practice downstream tasks with minimal human supervision.  Experiments on real-world and simulated robot embodiments demonstrate that this combination of SFT and Self-Improvement is more sample-efficient and achieves higher success rates than scaling imitation data alone.  The method also unlocks a novel capability: autonomous practice and acquisition of novel skills that generalize beyond the imitation learning dataset used during training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *combination* of existing ideas in a new context, robotics. Specifically:
    *   Applying RL-style post-training to EFMs (inspired by LLM literature) is a natural but previously unexplored direction. The "steps-to-go" prediction objective for reward function extraction is a smart, but not groundbreaking, technique.
    *   The framework's demonstration of sample efficiency is an important empirical finding.
    *   Enabling generalization to *new* skills, not just semantic generalization of existing ones, is a significant contribution. It highlights a unique advantage gained from the combined pre-training and online self-improvement approach.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it addresses a major limitation of current EFMs – reliance on supervised learning and behavior cloning. By introducing a practical way to incorporate reinforcement learning, the paper points towards more autonomous and adaptable robot learning systems. The demonstration of learning entirely *new* skills extends the potential of foundation models in robotics beyond simply mimicking existing behaviors.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation (bridging the gap between LLM and robotic training paradigms).
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation with both real-world and simulated robots, increasing credibility.
    *   Ablation studies clearly demonstrate the importance of each component (pre-training, SFT, Self-Improvement).
    *   Clear and concise writing.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The "steps-to-go" reward function, while effective, is not deeply novel and could be sensitive to the accuracy of the EFM. Alternative reward shaping approaches were not explored in depth.
    *   The implementation (PaLI model, training details) is complex and might be a barrier to wider adoption, especially given computational costs. Simpler architectures could have been more impactful for accessibility.
    *   While the paper shows generalization to *new* skills, the complexity of these skills (BananaTable task) might be relatively limited. A more extensive evaluation on diverse and challenging real-world tasks would further strengthen the claims.
    *   The paper highlights impressive success, but it would benefit from discussing failure cases and limitations of the approach more critically. Potential failure modes were not explored or demonstrated.
    *   The reward and policy training process may have limitations in the diversity of behaviors that the robots can learn, particularly given the lack of exploration of different reward definitions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on EFM post-training and inspire further exploration of RL-based approaches for robot learning. It provides a strong empirical case for combining pre-trained foundation models with online self-improvement to create more adaptable and autonomous robots.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has notable novelty and significance. The application of RL-inspired methods to EFMs, and the ability to learn *new* skills from online interactions, are important contributions.  However, the core algorithm for rewarding the agent, while practical and sound, seems to rely on a relatively simple estimation for success (steps-to-go prediction). This, combined with complexities involved with setting up and training the EFMs, significantly limits the scope of the paper. A lot of the benefits seem to be coming out of training an EFM on diverse amounts of data instead of anything truly algorithmically groundbreaking. The experimental rigour is solid, but potential failures are not discussed, reducing the impact.  It pushes the field forward but with incremental, not revolutionary steps.

Score: 6

The paper presents a clear and significant advancement in EFM training but it does not revolutionize the field of RL robotics in its entirety, hence it is not rated any higher than a score of '6'.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Mind the Gap: Data Rewriting for Stable Off-Policy Supervised Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15157v1)
- **Authors**: Shiwan Zhao, Xuyang Zhao, Jiaming Zhou, Aobo Kong, Qicheng Li, Yong Qin
- **Abstract**: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) of large language models can be viewed as an off-policy learning problem, where expert demonstrations come from a fixed behavior policy while training aims to optimize a target policy. Importance sampling is the standard tool for correcting this distribution mismatch, but large policy gaps lead to high variance and training instability. Existing approaches mitigate this issue using KL penalties or clipping, which passively constrain updates rather than actively reducing the gap. We propose a simple yet effective data rewriting framework that proactively shrinks the policy gap by keeping correct solutions as on-policy data and rewriting incorrect ones with guided re-solving, falling back to expert demonstrations only when needed. This aligns the training distribution with the target policy before optimization, reducing importance sampling variance and stabilizing off-policy fine-tuning. Experiments on five mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate consistent and significant gains over both vanilla SFT and the state-of-the-art Dynamic Fine-Tuning (DFT) approach. The data and code will be released at https://github.com/NKU-HLT/Off-Policy-SFT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Mind the Gap: Data Rewriting for Stable Off-Policy Supervised Fine-Tuning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of instability in supervised fine-tuning (SFT) of large language models (LLMs), which they frame as an off-policy learning problem. The core idea is to proactively reduce the policy gap between the fixed expert demonstrations (behavior policy) and the evolving model policy by rewriting the SFT dataset. The proposed method, called data rewriting, involves a three-stage process: (1) self-alignment (sampling and retaining correct responses generated directly by the model), (2) guided-alignment (re-prompting with a reference solution for incorrect responses to generate digest-and-retell responses), and (3) fallback (using the original expert demonstration when both alignment attempts fail).  This rewritten dataset, combined with importance sampling (IS) during training, is shown to stabilize off-policy fine-tuning and improve performance on mathematical reasoning benchmarks compared to vanilla SFT and Dynamic Fine-Tuning (DFT).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in framing SFT as an off-policy learning problem and addressing it with a data rewriting framework. While the concept of using IS to correct for distributional shifts is well-established, the idea of *proactively* reducing the policy gap *before* optimization by rewriting the data is a valuable contribution. The digest-and-retell prompting strategy adds a further layer of refinement to this approach.  The ablation studies provide insight into the importance of each stage. Prior work has certainly looked at data-centric improvements for SFT, but this approach to *dynamically rewrite* the data to align with the target policy is relatively novel.

*   **Significance:** Stabilizing SFT is a crucial step towards making LLM fine-tuning more robust and reliable. Given that SFT is often used as a precursor to more complex techniques like RLHF, improving its stability has a cascading effect. The gains demonstrated on mathematical reasoning benchmarks are significant and consistent, suggesting that the approach is effective. The experimental results showing consistent gains on several mathematical reasoning benchmarks significantly strengthen the paper. Addressing the policy gap in SFT is highly significant. While RL avoids this gap by being on-policy, SFT remains a vital tool due to its simplicity and ease of incorporating external knowledge.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear framing of the problem (SFT as off-policy learning).
    *   Well-motivated and intuitive data rewriting framework.
    *   Empirically validated with strong results on multiple benchmarks.
    *   Ablation studies provide insight into component effectiveness.
    *   Analysis of training dynamics and log-probabilities offers useful information.
    *   The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Evaluation is limited to mathematical reasoning. While this domain is challenging, it would be beneficial to see results on other types of tasks (e.g., code generation, text summarization).
    *   The computational cost of the data rewriting step could be a concern, especially for very large datasets or complex models. This aspect could be discussed more explicitly in the paper. Sampling 10 candidate solutions for each instance may be prohibitive in some scenarios.
    *   The improvement is less pronounced on the instruction-tuned Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct model compared to the base Qwen2.5-Math-7B model, suggesting the approach might be less effective when the base model already has strong instruction following capabilities.
    *   The paper mentions online data rewriting as future work, and this would indeed be a valuable direction to explore. The current approach is offline and therefore doesn't fully address the policy gap that emerges during training.
    *   The approach to approximate the denominator of the importance weights by setting it to 1 might introduce some bias.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by highlighting the importance of addressing the policy gap in SFT and by providing a practical and effective method for doing so. The data rewriting framework could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners to improve the stability and performance of their LLM fine-tuning pipelines. The conceptual framing is insightful and useful for the community.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM fine-tuning by framing SFT as an off-policy learning problem and proposing a data rewriting framework to address the policy gap. The empirical results are strong and consistent, demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach. However, the limitations related to the scope of evaluation (mathematical reasoning only), potential computational cost, and the less pronounced gains on instruction-tuned models prevent it from receiving a higher score. Also, approximations used for importance sampling and offline nature of the framework limit the impact. Overall, it's a valuable addition to the literature, but with some caveats.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### AIP: Subverting Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Adversarial Instructional Prompt
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15159v1)
- **Authors**: Saket S. Chaturvedi, Gaurav Bagwe, Lan Zhang, Xiaoyong Yuan
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances large language models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant documents from external sources to improve factual accuracy and verifiability. However, this reliance introduces new attack surfaces within the retrieval pipeline, beyond the LLM itself. While prior RAG attacks have exposed such vulnerabilities, they largely rely on manipulating user queries, which is often infeasible in practice due to fixed or protected user inputs. This narrow focus overlooks a more realistic and stealthy vector: instructional prompts, which are widely reused, publicly shared, and rarely audited. Their implicit trust makes them a compelling target for adversaries to manipulate RAG behavior covertly. We introduce a novel attack for Adversarial Instructional Prompt (AIP) that exploits adversarial instructional prompts to manipulate RAG outputs by subtly altering retrieval behavior. By shifting the attack surface to the instructional prompts, AIP reveals how trusted yet seemingly benign interface components can be weaponized to degrade system integrity. The attack is crafted to achieve three goals: (1) naturalness, to evade user detection; (2) utility, to encourage use of prompts; and (3) robustness, to remain effective across diverse query variations. We propose a diverse query generation strategy that simulates realistic linguistic variation in user queries, enabling the discovery of prompts that generalize across paraphrases and rephrasings. Building on this, a genetic algorithm-based joint optimization is developed to evolve adversarial prompts by balancing attack success, clean-task utility, and stealthiness. Experimental results show that AIP achieves up to 95.23% ASR while preserving benign functionality. These findings uncover a critical and previously overlooked vulnerability in RAG systems, emphasizing the need to reassess the shared instructional prompts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AIP: Subverting Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Adversarial Instructional Prompt":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel attack, named Adversarial Instructional Prompt (AIP), against Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems.  Instead of manipulating user queries or directly accessing the LLM, AIP crafts subtly malicious *instructional prompts*, which are commonly used templates shared publicly and incorporated into user queries. These crafted prompts manipulate the retrieval behavior of the RAG system, causing it to surface adversarial documents and bias the final output.  The attack is designed to be natural, useful for benign tasks, and robust to variations in user queries. The authors propose a three-stage attack framework: prompt/document initialization, diverse query generation (to simulate realistic user behavior), and adversarial joint optimization (using a genetic algorithm) to refine the prompt and documents.  Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of AIP, achieving high attack success rates while preserving clean-task performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in shifting the attack surface from user queries (which are often protected) to instructional prompts.  While attacks on RAG systems exist, the AIP approach targets a less-explored and potentially more vulnerable component of the architecture. Exploiting the trust placed in shared instructional prompts is a significant contribution. Prior attacks relied on more explicit user manipulation or access to internal system components. This focus on instruction prompt injection is a valuable and original contribution to security research in LLM systems.

*   **Significance:** This work highlights a critical vulnerability in RAG systems that has significant practical implications. The widespread use of shared instructional prompts makes this attack scalable and difficult to detect.  The paper demonstrates a realistic threat model, where adversaries can covertly inject bias into RAG systems without requiring access to the LLM or direct user manipulation. The high success rates reported (up to 95.23%) underscores the severity of the vulnerability. The insights presented in the post-hoc analysis offer valuable guidance for developing more robust RAG systems. The work significantly contributes to our understanding of RAG vulnerabilities and has the potential to influence future research in secure LLM deployments.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem statement and clear articulation of the threat model.
    *   Novel attack surface and a practical approach that does not require access to model internals.
    *   The three-stage framework is well-designed and addresses the key challenges of naturalness, utility, and robustness.
    *   The use of diverse query generation is a strength, improving the realism and generalizability of the attack.
    *   The experiments are thorough and demonstrate the effectiveness of AIP against strong baselines.
    *   The post-hoc analysis provides valuable insights into the limitations and potential improvements of the attack.
    *   Addresses real-world deployment concerns effectively.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation of "naturalness" relies primarily on LLM-based judgments, which can be subjective.  Including human evaluations could strengthen this aspect of the paper.  Although GRUEN score is included, direct human assessment would be more powerful.
    *   While the paper discusses potential defense strategies, it does not propose or evaluate any specific defenses against AIP. Further work could focus on developing practical mitigation techniques.
    *   The method is designed to attack specific documents, so it is only feasible when an attacker aims at specific information and controls the creation of the documents.

*   **Justification for Score:**  The paper is a significant contribution due to its novelty in shifting the attack surface to instructional prompts, its practical threat model, and its demonstration of high attack success rates. The identified vulnerability in RAG systems is important and merits attention from both researchers and practitioners. While there are some limitations in the evaluation of naturalness and the absence of proposed defenses, the overall contribution is substantial and will likely stimulate further research in this area. Given the novelty of instruction prompt injections, the significance of identifying a threat vector in a commonly used LLM deployment approach, and the high degree of success that this technique achieves, a high score is merited.
