### An Evaluation-Centric Paradigm for Scientific Visualization Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15160v1)
- **Authors**: Kuangshi Ai, Haichao Miao, Zhimin Li, Chaoli Wang, Shusen Liu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) have enabled increasingly sophisticated autonomous visualization agents capable of translating user intentions into data visualizations. However, measuring progress and comparing different agents remains challenging, particularly in scientific visualization (SciVis), due to the absence of comprehensive, large-scale benchmarks for evaluating real-world capabilities. This position paper examines the various types of evaluation required for SciVis agents, outlines the associated challenges, provides a simple proof-of-concept evaluation example, and discusses how evaluation benchmarks can facilitate agent self-improvement. We advocate for a broader collaboration to develop a SciVis agentic evaluation benchmark that would not only assess existing capabilities but also drive innovation and stimulate future development in the field.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the need for standardized evaluation benchmarks for scientific visualization (SciVis) agents powered by multi-modal large language models (MLLMs).  The authors argue that current evaluation methods are inadequate for the complexities of SciVis, which involves sophisticated data transformations, diverse rendering techniques, and intricate parameter mappings. They propose an "evaluation-centric" paradigm, advocating for evaluation to drive agent design, not just validate it.  The paper presents a taxonomy for SciVis agent evaluation, categorizing tasks into outcome-based and process-based assessments.  It offers a proof-of-concept evaluation example using the Bonsai dataset and ParaView, demonstrating how to combine multi-modal LLM judging with hard-coded verifiers and system efficiency metrics.  The authors call for broader collaboration to develop comprehensive SciVis agent evaluation benchmarks that will foster innovation and reliable deployment of these agents in scientific applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its framing of evaluation as a core driver for *design* rather than just assessment, a crucial and often overlooked element in the realm of LLM and AI-powered scientific applications. While some elements, like LLM judges and automated verification, are present in other agent evaluations, applying them *specifically* to the complexities of SciVis and combining these evaluation methods in a unified manner is reasonably novel. The outcome/process based classification itself is not particularly novel, but it's effective in structuring the problem.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is substantial and multi-faceted:

    *   **Addressing a Critical Gap:** It directly tackles a recognized problem: the lack of rigorous, standardized evaluation for increasingly sophisticated SciVis agents.
    *   **Promoting Reliable Deployment:** By emphasizing evaluation-driven design, the paper aims to increase the reliability and reproducibility of SciVis agents, which are crucial for their adoption in scientific workflows where accuracy is paramount.
    *   **Catalyzing Community Effort:** The paper explicitly calls for community collaboration, a necessary step to build comprehensive benchmarks covering the diversity of SciVis tasks and tools. This call is likely to resonate with the community and encourage further development in this area.
    *   **Driving Innovation:** By identifying clear evaluation targets and providing actionable feedback, the proposed benchmarks have the potential to accelerate innovation in SciVis agent development.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly defines the problem of inadequate SciVis agent evaluation and articulates the need for a new paradigm.
    *   **Clear Taxonomy:** The proposed taxonomy provides a useful framework for organizing evaluation tasks.
    *   **Practical Example:** The Bonsai dataset example helps to ground the discussion and demonstrates how the evaluation framework can be implemented in practice.
    *   **Actionable Recommendations:** The paper offers concrete recommendations for developing evaluation benchmarks, including incorporating multifaceted metrics, spanning diverse tasks, and addressing cost-effectiveness.
    *   **Clear Call for Collaboration:** The explicit call for community collaboration is a strong point, increasing the likelihood of real-world impact.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** While the paper addresses a critical need, the proof-of-concept is still preliminary. The example could benefit from a more exhaustive treatment and a wider range of scenarios.
    *   **Specific Metric Choices:** The paper uses several types of metrics but lacks a formal justification for the selection of these, which undermines the reliability of the proposed evaluation. More discussion is warranted about the tradeoffs between them.
    *   **Lack of Generalizability:** The example is focused on ParaView. The paper could benefit from outlining a plan for generalizability to other SciVis tools (napari, VMD, etc.).
    *   **Safety Concerns:** While the authors mention the safety concern it can be more emphasized. More concrete solutions should be addressed to prevent any accidental deployment in production systems.
    *   **Over-reliance on LLMs:** There are well-known limits to LLM judges, especially regarding visual grounding.  The paper acknowledges this but could explore alternative or complementary evaluation strategies in greater depth.
    *   **Depth of Process Evaluation:** Process evaluation is essential, but more discussion is needed on *how* to systematically analyze the complex action sequences agents take.  What kinds of reasoning failures are most common? How can those be detected and quantified programmatically?
* **Justification:**
This paper addresses a significant gap in evaluating SciVis agents and proposes a valuable evaluation-centric paradigm. The framework is well-structured and offers a practical example using Bonsai to test the effectiveness of SciVis agents. However, several limitations are present, including a limited scope and the over-reliance on LLMs for judgment.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is highly likely to influence the field by promoting a more rigorous and standardized approach to SciVis agent evaluation. The call for collaboration is particularly important.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a valuable concept and offers a well-structured taxonomy. The significance of addressing the evaluation gap in SciVis cannot be understated. However, the lack of validation across multiple datasets, agent architectures and concerns of safety detract from an otherwise excellent paper.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Watermarking and Anomaly Detection in Machine Learning Models for LORA RF Fingerprinting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15170v1)
- **Authors**: Aarushi Mahajan, Wayne Burleson
- **Abstract**: Radio frequency fingerprint identification (RFFI) distinguishes wireless devices by the small variations in their analog circuits, avoiding heavy cryptographic authentication. While deep learning on spectrograms improves accuracy, models remain vulnerable to copying, tampering, and evasion. We present a stronger RFFI system combining watermarking for ownership proof and anomaly detection for spotting suspicious inputs. Using a ResNet-34 on log-Mel spectrograms, we embed three watermarks: a simple trigger, an adversarially trained trigger robust to noise and filtering, and a hidden gradient/weight signature. A convolutional Variational Autoencoders (VAE) with Kullback-Leibler (KL) warm-up and free-bits flags off-distribution queries. On the LoRa dataset, our system achieves 94.6% accuracy, 98% watermark success, and 0.94 AUROC, offering verifiable, tamper-resistant authentication.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a Radio Frequency Fingerprint Identification (RFFI) system designed to be more robust against adversarial attacks and model theft.  It combines a ResNet-34 classifier (operating on log-Mel spectrograms of LoRa transmissions) with three types of watermarks (a simple trigger, an adversarially trained trigger, and a hidden gradient/weight signature) and a convolutional variational autoencoder (VAE) as an anomaly detector or "guard." The watermarks protect against model copying, input evasion, and weight modifications, while the VAE flags suspicious inputs that deviate from normal device patterns.  Experimental results on a LoRa dataset demonstrate that the system achieves high classification accuracy, watermark verification success, and anomaly detection performance, suggesting improved security and reliability compared to previous RFFI approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the *integration* of multiple defense mechanisms. While individual components like using CNNs for RFFI, watermarking deep learning models, and using autoencoders for anomaly detection are not entirely new, their combined application within the RFFI domain *is* a contribution. The paper makes use of a variety of different techniques and combines them to deliver a strong RFII system.

*   **Significance:** The significance is in addressing a key vulnerability in RFFI systems: their susceptibility to adversarial attacks and model theft. By demonstrating a practical system that integrates defenses against these threats, the paper takes a step toward making RFFI more viable for real-world deployments in security-sensitive IoT applications. RFFI systems that can resist attacks are highly valuable for the world's security.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper tackles multiple threat vectors (model theft, weight tampering, input evasion) with a multi-layered defense strategy.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The system is evaluated on a real-world LoRa dataset, demonstrating the practical effectiveness of the proposed techniques.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the different components and their interactions.
    *   **Detailed Methodology:** They explain the process, from the formation of spectrograms to the training and development of the model.
    *   **Detailed Comparison:** The methodology details different approaches and comparisons for the autoencoder and other aspects.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** As noted above, the individual components are not revolutionary. The novelty is more in the integration and application to RFFI.
    *   **Limited Adversarial Evaluation:** While the paper evaluates the robustness of the adversarial trigger, a more comprehensive analysis of the system's resilience to a wider range of sophisticated attacks would strengthen the claims. For instance, it's unclear how the system would perform against adaptive attacks specifically designed to circumvent the combined defenses.
    *   **Dataset Specificity:** The evaluation is limited to a single LoRa dataset.  Generalizability to other RF technologies and environments needs to be considered.
    *   **Scalability Concerns**: The paper lacks analysis of its scalability implications. The computational cost of the proposed scheme in realistic settings with a large number of devices is not assessed.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper could inspire further research into robust RFFI techniques. The combination of watermarking and anomaly detection represents a promising direction. It also highlights the importance of considering security aspects early in the design of RFFI systems.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the integration of defenses is valuable, the relatively incremental nature of the individual components and the limitations in adversarial evaluation temper the overall impact. The work takes an important step towards secure RFFI, but further validation and exploration of advanced attack scenarios are needed.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### SMARTER: A Data-efficient Framework to Improve Toxicity Detection with Explanation via Self-augmenting Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15174v1)
- **Authors**: Huy Nghiem, Advik Sachdeva, Hal Daumé III
- **Abstract**: WARNING: This paper contains examples of offensive materials. Toxic content has become pervasive on social media platforms. We introduce SMARTER, a data-efficient two-stage framework for explainable content moderation using Large Language Models (LLMs). In Stage 1, we leverage LLMs' own outputs to generate synthetic explanations for both correct and incorrect labels, enabling alignment via preference optimization with minimal human supervision. In Stage 2, we refine explanation quality through cross-model training, allowing weaker models to align stylistically and semantically with stronger ones. Experiments on three benchmark tasks -- HateXplain, Latent Hate, and Implicit Hate -- demonstrate that SMARTER enables LLMs to achieve up to a 13.5% macro-F1 improvement over standard few-shot baselines while using only a fraction of the full training data. Our framework offers a scalable strategy for low-resource settings by harnessing LLMs' self-improving capabilities for both classification and explanation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SMARTER, a data-efficient framework for improving toxicity detection with explanation using Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework operates in two stages: (1) individual LLM self-augmentation, where each LLM generates synthetic explanations for both correct and incorrect labels and aligns using preference optimization, and (2) cross-model refinement, where one LLM is trained with responses generated by another LLM on a held-out set to improve explanation quality. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of SMARTER on three benchmark tasks (HateXplain, Latent Hate, and Implicit Hate) using open-source LLMs (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and COT-T5-XL). The experiments show that SMARTER achieves improved macro-F1 scores with a fraction of the full training data and offers a scalable strategy for low-resource settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strength:** The core idea of leveraging LLMs to self-augment data with synthetic explanations, especially conditioned on both correct and incorrect labels, and then using preference optimization is a valuable approach for addressing data scarcity in toxicity detection. The cross-model refinement stage adds another layer of improvement by allowing models to learn from each other's generated explanations, enhancing the overall quality.
    *   **Weakness:** Self-augmentation has been explored in other contexts. The preference optimization and cross model training are the differentiating factors.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strength:** The paper addresses a crucial issue in content moderation – the need for data-efficient methods that provide both accurate classification and intuitive explanations. The framework's scalability and practicality make it potentially useful for low-resource NLP practitioners and social media platforms dealing with limited data or nuanced toxic concepts. The experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate tangible improvements, suggesting the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Weakness:** The focus on only two open-source LLMs (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and COT-T5-XL) could limit the generalizability of the findings. The lack of extensive human evaluation (limited to HateXplain) also raises questions about the perceived quality and trustworthiness of the generated explanations across different datasets. The discussion of architectural diversity is somewhat superficial, and it would be useful to see a deeper exploration of how different model architectures contribute to the framework's performance. The ethical considerations, while mentioned, are not thoroughly examined.

*   **Detailed Justification for Score:**

    The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of toxicity detection and content moderation by introducing a practical and scalable framework that leverages LLMs for self-augmentation and cross-model refinement. The empirical results on benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of SMARTER, achieving improved classification performance and offering interpretable explanations. However, the limitations regarding model selection, human evaluation, architectural diversity, and ethical considerations prevent a higher score.

### Unleashing the Potential of Multimodal LLMs for Zero-Shot Spatio-Temporal Video Grounding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15178v1)
- **Authors**: Zaiquan Yang, Yuhao Liu, Gerhard Hancke, Rynson W. H. Lau
- **Abstract**: Spatio-temporal video grounding (STVG) aims at localizing the spatio-temporal tube of a video, as specified by the input text query. In this paper, we utilize multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to explore a zero-shot solution in STVG. We reveal two key insights about MLLMs: (1) MLLMs tend to dynamically assign special tokens, referred to as \textit{grounding tokens}, for grounding the text query; and (2) MLLMs often suffer from suboptimal grounding due to the inability to fully integrate the cues in the text query (\textit{e.g.}, attributes, actions) for inference. Based on these insights, we propose a MLLM-based zero-shot framework for STVG, which includes novel decomposed spatio-temporal highlighting (DSTH) and temporal-augmented assembling (TAS) strategies to unleash the reasoning ability of MLLMs. The DSTH strategy first decouples the original query into attribute and action sub-queries for inquiring the existence of the target both spatially and temporally. It then uses a novel logit-guided re-attention (LRA) module to learn latent variables as spatial and temporal prompts, by regularizing token predictions for each sub-query. These prompts highlight attribute and action cues, respectively, directing the model's attention to reliable spatial and temporal related visual regions. In addition, as the spatial grounding by the attribute sub-query should be temporally consistent, we introduce the TAS strategy to assemble the predictions using the original video frames and the temporal-augmented frames as inputs to help improve temporal consistency. We evaluate our method on various MLLMs, and show that it outperforms SOTA methods on three common STVG benchmarks. The code will be available at https://github.com/zaiquanyang/LLaVA_Next_STVG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of zero-shot spatio-temporal video grounding (STVG) using multimodal large language models (MLLMs). It identifies two key insights: 1) MLLMs dynamically assign special tokens (grounding tokens) for grounding the text query, and 2) MLLMs can suffer from suboptimal grounding due to the inability to fully integrate all cues (attributes, actions) in the text query. Based on these insights, the paper proposes a framework that includes a decomposed spatio-temporal highlighting (DSTH) strategy and a temporal-augmented assembling (TAS) strategy. DSTH decouples the query into attribute and action sub-queries and uses a logit-guided re-attention (LRA) module to learn spatial and temporal prompts. TAS assembles predictions using the original and temporally-augmented frames to improve temporal consistency. The method is evaluated on STVG benchmarks and shows improved performance compared to state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:

    *   **Identification of Grounding Tokens:** The empirical finding that special tokens in MLLMs, specifically those following instructions, have a significant impact on grounding is a valuable observation. This observation shifts the focus from solely analyzing generated tokens to the broader context of special input tokens.
    *   **DSTH and LRA:** The decomposed spatio-temporal highlighting strategy, especially the Logit-guided Re-Attention (LRA) module, is a novel approach to prompt tuning in the zero-shot setting. Instead of directly fine-tuning the entire MLLM or relying on manually crafted prompts, the LRA adaptively learns visual prompts by regularizing token-level responses using sub-queries.
    *   **TAS:** The temporal-augmented assembling strategy to improve temporal consistency, given the reliance on attribute sub-queries, is a sound approach and contributes to improved performance.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Zero-Shot STVG:** The paper addresses the important problem of zero-shot STVG, reducing the need for costly frame-level annotations.
    *   **MLLM Utilization:** It makes good use of the strong cross-modal comprehension ability of MLLMs and contributes to the growing body of research on leveraging MLLMs for visual grounding.
    *   **Performance Gains:** The experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing zero-shot methods on multiple STVG benchmarks. This is particularly important, as many zero-shot methods still lag behind fully supervised approaches. The gains are not incremental; they are a notable jump.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem and motivation.
    *   **Well-Explained Methodology:** The proposed framework and its components (DSTH, LRA, TAS) are well-explained.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results are comprehensive and support the claims made in the paper. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Qualitative Results:** The qualitative results visually illustrate the effectiveness of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** While the zero-shot nature is a benefit, the paper acknowledges that MLLMs are computationally expensive, potentially limiting the applicability of the method to long videos. Further work on efficiency is needed. The LRA will likely slow down processing, though the experiments clearly show gains.
    *   **Dependence on Tracker:** The method relies on object track proposals generated by an external tracker. While the paper includes an ablation study on different trackers, the performance is still dependent on the quality of the tracker. It would be valuable to explore approaches that are more robust to noisy or inaccurate tracker outputs.
    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments:** While three datasets are used for benchmarking, the diversity of these datasets might be limited. Testing on more challenging and diverse datasets could further strengthen the claims.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The findings regarding grounding tokens and the LRA module could inspire further research on prompt tuning and attention mechanisms in MLLMs.
    *   The DSTH and TAS strategies could be adopted or adapted by other researchers working on zero-shot or weakly-supervised video grounding tasks.
    *   The paper's approach could potentially be extended to other vision-language tasks that require fine-grained spatio-temporal reasoning.

*   **Justification for Score:**
This paper introduces a new and effective framework for the challenging task of zero-shot STVG by leveraging the inherent strengths of MLLMs in a novel way. The identification of grounding tokens, the decomposed spatio-temporal highlighting strategy with the LRA module, and the temporal-augmented assembling strategy are all sound contributions. While the paper is not without limitations, it addresses a key problem with impressive results, laying a foundation for further research. The performance improvement is notable, showing a meaningful jump over SOTA.
